ParlaMint-DK_20200514105430	Thank you for the floor. When I had to write this speech today, it was a little bit of a reflection on how to open up. For how do I at the same time say that today is a day of joy because we are dealing first with the Climate Act, but also that it is a very, very sad period in both the history of the world and the history of Denmark, because the Coronapandemic, the economic downturn and the climate crisis together are beginning to draw to a perfect storm. What kind of leg do you have to stand on? But maybe it's not so hard to find a grimass that can fit, for whatever crisis we're facing is whether it's a health crisis, an economic crisis or the climate crisis... then rarely does anything good come out of black vision and pessimism. In fact, I think it is absolutely crucial that we keep the hope that we can tackle the challenges we face; the hope that we can get through and beyond, further out of the health crisis, further out of the economic crisis created by decommissioning, and also further out of the climate crisis. For without that hope we risk losing the political momentum we need to solve the problems we are constantly facing. That is why I believe that pessimism is a kind of luxury that we cannot afford right now. Because if climate change was a major societal challenge before the Coronapandemic broke out, then it is certain that they represent a huge challenge now, too, here after the crisis and during the crisis. And this is because the corona virus has, after all, weakened one of the very, very potent and best means we have to deal with climate change: a strong economy both in companies and in society. Because, as we see in the Left, the green transition and a healthy economy are two sides of the same coin. If the economy is not moving, if new technology is not being developed and climate-friendly ideas are not being devised in companies, then the green transition will go all too slowly, and then the transition will be overtaken within climate change. Does this mean that we in the Left believe that the economic downturn that the Coronapandemic has started is more important than the climate crisis? No, but it is just important to understand that the Corona crisis and the climate crisis are interfering with each other and that the Corona crisis exacerbates the situation because the Corona virus puts the world economy and hence the green transition to a halt. In the very short term, therefore, we need to reopen society. We need to get the economy going, production going, and we need to increase activity. And on that a little longer track, we need climate action plans, because otherwise the bill we are discussing today will not be worth more than the paper on which it is written. And that means listening to business recommendations, to researchers and to the Climate Council, but that also means we have to get started. This also means that under no circumstances should we compromise on the 70-percent objective, even because of the corona virus. Because, as a good friend of mine has sometimes said: when it comes to prioritising, it is sometimes the case that what is urgent is shouting and what is important is whispering. And the coronapandemic is yelling. The goddamn virus is gallping up, and of course it must have that attention, but we cannot allow climate change to slip out of the field of vision, even if it is perhaps only whispering so far. Today, I hope that we will take the first step towards promising the focus on climate change. Because with the Climate Act, which we are adopting today, we are making demands on ourselves. We undertake to follow up and evaluate the climate action we are doing. We are being forced to set new ambitious targets. Finally, we also ensure that the current government sets climate targets with a 10-year aim every five years. We think this is both good and ambitious and we owe it to the climate, and we owe it to future generations. That is why, of course, the Left is 100% behind this bill and we look forward to the committee reading and discussion there. That being said, it is therefore more important than ever that we begin to see the economy and the green transition as each other's prerequisites and not each other's contradictions. Less activity and production are not the answer that the climate issue lacks. As there was a slightly confused movie rock that wrote in Politiken back in March, we should not want a corona epidemic every year, where everything stops, so that we can comply with the Paris Agreement. No, we have to do exactly the opposite. We need to get the economy going, get the good green ideas going and get climate creativity going. And when, at some point, we are going to give the economy a political backlash, let us do it in a green direction. Let us try to see the Corona crisis as not only a setback, but also an opportunity. Let us see this as an opportunity for the green transition to get a boost there too. And then let us give the economy and the Danish companies who make a living developing green technology the support they need so badly right now. As I said at the beginning, the time is not for black vision, but on the other hand it is not for jubilee. And so we must not forget that the only thing free of charge at a windmill is the wind that makes the wings spin. We must not forget that development requires profits, that innovation requires investment and that the fight for the climate requires capital. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20200514123449	Thank you for the floor. Today we are discussing a Climate Act for Denmark. The Act states that Denmark must reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 70% by 2030. in relation to 1990 levels. It also states that Denmark must be a pioneering country in international climate action: We can inspire and influence the rest of the world. So we have to make a 70-percent reduction for Denmark's geographical area. A reduction in Denmark's emissions will have no effect on the world's total CO2 emissions. Denmark's emissions are only 0.1 per cent. of the world's total emissions. Even if we stopped with all emissions in Denmark tomorrow, it would have no effect on the climate at all. The only purpose of the law must therefore be to be a pioneering country. But why should Denmark be a pioneering country? Why should we go so far ahead of all other countries? It is fine that Denmark lowers its CO2 emissions, and we have also been good at that so far, but putting such a rigid target on our own will make us shoot ourselves in the foot. The Climate Act was a really bad idea before the corona crisis, and it is an even worse idea today. We do not need to climb the money in this crisis situation. We've got plenty more to spend on. Nevertheless, the left in particular is pressing for reductions to be speeded up by means of a climate law. Have they not discovered that we are in the midst of a severe economic and health crisis? I do not think the world is particularly concerned with what we in Denmark are doing in this area. That they should be inspired by us is a fantasy that can cost us dearly. And is there such an urgent need for this Climate Act right now? I believe that the rest of the world is more concerned with corona right now and that it will be more concerned with economic recovery when the health crisis is over. Lowering Denmark's economic growth is also the wrong way to tackle the problem. Only a prosperous country in economic prosperity will be able to afford new and cleaner technologies. Therefore, a climate policy that weakens economic growth and job creation in Denmark will do more harm than it will benefit. This is true in general, especially in the situation we are currently in. Besides making us poorer, it also leads to what is called leakage. For example, if we reduce our agricultural or industrial production in Denmark because it will be too difficult to compete, this does not mean that production will disappear. The demand is still there, the goods are simply being produced in another country. Thus global emissions are not reduced by 70 per cent. of Denmark's discharge. A large part of the emissions can move to other countries, and this means that the total emissions are not reduced nearly as much. It makes no sense to focus unequivocally on the Danish emissions. Reduction in emissions must take place globally, otherwise it will have no effect. This is not related at all to the fact that we in Denmark then lower our emissions by 70%. with high costs. More expensive. Is that what Denmark has to take the lead in? That's absurd. After all, there are no countries in the world that would just consider introducing it, let alone poor countries. It has five points: (1) implementing a green tax reform that puts a high price on climate gases; (2) boosting the development of renewable energy and the phasing-out of fossil energy; (3) all support schemes must be based on green criteria, including for agriculture and also for construction; (4) the transport must release the bond to fossil energy; and (5) nature and agriculture must be given priority hand in hand, for example. with less meat production and more untouched forests. ParlaMint-DK_20200514125437	Thank you, President. Normally, when politicians set objectives for themselves and for our country, it is about the case that analyses are made of the various instruments available and, above all, analyses of the resources available. Then it will have to be reversed in the Ministry of Finance, and then there will probably be a slight reduction in the level of ambition, and then it will have to be negotiated in the Folketing, and then one and the other will have to be taken into account. Finally, a compromise is reached. Politics is the art of the possible. You do what's possible. This time, with this bill, we kind of turn it around: we don't start by asking what's possible. We start by asking: What is necessary? That's what makes these 70%. So groundbreaking. That is why the rest of the world looks at Denmark and says: You are a pioneering country. It's you we should follow. The 70%. It's not an arbitrary number. It is a figure based on an analysis of what Denmark should do if we are to live up to it as probably the only country in the world at present, hopefully not at all the last of the Paris Agreement. Does that mean we know exactly how we're going to get there? No, that's exactly the point: we choose to commit ourselves to this, despite the fact that we are actually at the moment. do not have clear answers to exactly what it will cost, clear answers to exactly how we will get there. But we're gonna do it anyway. Why are we doing this? Yeah, because we're a small country here. Had we been China, the best thing we could do was to concentrate very unilaterally on our own energy system and our own policy and thus actually pull some CO2 out of the atmosphere. But because we only stand for 0.1%. of the world's greenhouse gas emissions, it will probably not mean much to the climate if we then closed the country tomorrow. What we can do is first of all that we can inspire. We can show the rest of the world, those who really deduce that one can be a rich country and can well be a country with competitiveness, can well be a country that creates growth and jobs, can well be a country where citizens have a high standard of living, while doing these things. That's what we have to show. Secondly, when we set ourselves objectives that are so difficult to achieve that we need to develop new methods, new ways of cooperation, new technology, it will mean that we will be the ones who deliver solutions to the rest of the world quite specifically. We've done this before, we've shown it before. Now we are in the midst of a terrible health crisis. It does not really tolerate comparison with other crises we have been in, but it is fair to say that this is not the first time that we have faced a crisis. This is not the first time we have had a crisis that has an economic impact on Denmark either. Many remember the oil crises of the 1970s. It was also crises that pushed our economy. It could also have been said that now is really not the time for green experiments, because we are under pressure on our economy. What was chosen at the time was to say: should we not make a strategy for what we should live on in the future, which makes us independent of fossil fuels? Shouldn't we? So, for example, Denmark. started by introducing far stricter requirements than the rest of the world for energy efficiency, and when we started by focusing on renewable energy, including especially sea wind, we did so on the basis of a green argument, yes, but, in fact, above all on the basis of economic arguments. Because it was an opportunity for us to make a strategy for what we should live on in the future. And it has to be now. The Corona crisis has made this more difficult... clear. There are things we would probably have done that we are not going to make clear now. There are sequences that we need to change clearly. However, to put in place a completely new strategy based on the fact that we now have to compete on using a lot of energy and that we have to compete on old technologies, that we have to move away from exploiting the strength we have in the world in relation to renewable energy, energy efficiency, etc., would be quite foolish, even for economic reasons. Let us take a concrete example: sea wind. When Denmark made the first offshore wind farm in the world in 1991, it was very expensive. I think economists have torn themselves apart in the Ministry of Finance and elsewhere. But we did it anyway because we knew that yes, there are some who have to make the first expensive investments so that they can eventually become cheaper. Today we have tens of thousands of jobs in the wind turbine sector, and today it is a good business for us, obviously. So even in isolation, for our little country, it was a good idea. But look in the world. Look into the world where ocean winds can now compete at price with coal. I think one of the rapporteurs mentioned that India has now, for the first time, fallen in its CO2 emissions. Among other things, they do. due to a very offensive strategy for renewable energy, including much wind. Would it have happened if it had not been for Denmark? You can't know that. You have to be careful about such counter-productive history. But what we can know with one hundred percent certainty • what also Fatih Birol, the director of the International Energy Agency, said when he was in Copenhagen last year to present a report is that if Denmark hadn't gone ahead at that time, we wouldn't have been where we are now. For the fact that we took leadership there, the fact that we chose to prioritise, has simply changed how the whole world today has access to renewable energy. Thank you to the 68 000 people who have signed a citizens' proposal that has helped put this on the agenda. Thank you to the more than 160 seats in the Folketing, which will back this up. I can assure you that it is being noticed in the world. I have the honour to travel around on behalf of all of us, on behalf of this country, and lead the argument. And what I'm almost always faced with is: 70%, well! You can certainly see that there has been a social democratic government in your country. But what if you lose power? I have to say that we are proud that we have the power as Social Democrats, but this is something that largely the whole parliament is behind. I have even talked to my former colleague in the European Parliament about it, and they say: yes, yes, the whole Danish Parliament, but certainly not your good old friend Morten Messerschmidt; after all, not the Danish People's Party. Despite a bit of theatrical order today, they will probably end up voting in favour, so I can actually say to even the most right-wing forces in the European Parliament: yes, even the Danish People's Party. And what about industry? they say so. Are you wearing bodyguards now? You should. There are people who really have money at stake and who do not want this to happen under any circumstances. Well, Danish Industry backs up. Even those parts of industry that may not live directly on green solutions, which actually live directly on solutions that are far from green, can see that a future growth strategy for our country can only be based on being those that are at the forefront of this area. And it is quite beautiful that we can draw up an economic strategy for our country, which is the most sensible. It's not gonna be easy, it's gonna be expensive, we're gonna make mistakes and all that. But that's the most sensible thing. At the same time, we can be idealists, and at the same time we can show the world that, in the midst of this time when there is so much to worry about, there is also a country that is sticking to its objectives. We could not have foreseen the Corona crisis, some say, and that is why we must lower our level of ambition. No, that is true; we could not have foreseen it. But we can probably predict that between now and 2050, as this law does, there will of course be financial crises. And that is why we have written in some principles that ensure that we are not just forced to do this in a way that may be inappropriate. For, of course, if we want to be a pioneer country, we must also be aware that there is not much pioneering country and a good example in closing industry and moving it abroad. Then one would rightly be able to look at Denmark and say: Yes, you are an example, but you are a bad example. See, that's what happens if you set yourself too ambitious targets! We're finally not gonna do that. Therefore, it is so important that we insist that this does not wind up Danish economy, but develops Danish economy. That this does not lead to a lower standard of living, but to a better standard of living, that this basically makes Denmark a better country, and I believe it does. So thank you to all the parties that have been involved. I can honestly say that I always think it is, but it is probably not so often that it is said honestly that the law we are adopting now is much, much better than the original proposal put forward by the government. At least I'd like to say that. And this is simply because we had good, real, factual discussions, which were not about teasing each other, which were not about political cashiers, and there was a bit of that in the press. But when we sat at the table, it was a huge pleasure. So thank you very much to everyone who was involved in that work. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20200514132830	After the Danish Parliament changed the rules for self-supporting language students in 2018, when the participation fee was introduced, there has been a large decrease in the number of self-supporting students attending Danish education. The participation fee was introduced with the aim of ensuring high motivation among the self-supporting students who enrolled in Danish education, and this is of course important, but if the students do not sign up, then we have a completely different problem. It is important that foreigners working in Denmark learn to speak Danish. Danish employers will inevitably benefit more from foreign employees who master the Danish language. At the same time, foreign employees will be better placed to have a good relationship with colleagues, neighbours and friends while living in Denmark. At the same time, in line with more business organisations, we hope that it will help to maintain more foreign labour in Denmark than is the case today if people learn Danish. Therefore, this bill will remove the participant payment. On the other hand, we raise the deposit to participate in Danish education for self-supporting students. It will be raised from DKK 1,250. per module for 2,000 DKK. per module. After the new model, a student loses his or her deposit if he or she passes the module test later than the time limits applicable to each module. Each module shall, however, be passed within 6 months of the start of a course. This should ensure a high motivation among language students. Our expectation is that more people will learn Danish and take the teaching seriously. At the same time, we think it is quite reasonable for students to be met with a consequence if they do not fulfil the obligation to register for the course. Social democracy supports the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20200514135800	Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no brief comments to be made to the rapporteur. I would like to give the floor to the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration. Welcome.
ParlaMint-DK_20200514135811	Thank you. I would also like to start by thanking you for the receipt and debate on the draft law on the abolition of the participant payment and the amendment of the rules on the deposit. The participation fee for self-supporting students was introduced with effect from the summer of 2018, and the participation fee was introduced to ensure that only motivated students enrolled in Danish education. It also meant some more money in the Treasury, which at that time could be used, among other things. on tax reliefs. After the introduction of the participation fee, the number of self-supporting students attending Danish education is more than halved. The decline in the number of students has been significantly greater than was expected at the time. In the then bill, the Ministry estimated that the decrease would be 35%. less students, but the changes have meant 58 per cent. less students. And I have just been looking at the figures this morning, and it is especially in the figures for Eastern workers and students from other EU countries that there has been a rather marked decline. I believe that as a society we have a great interest in people coming to Denmark also learning Danish. It should not only be highly trained employees who may be able to get it paid by their employer, but also all other foreigners who fulfil an important function in the labour market and may have brought their family to Denmark. In this context, I also think it is worth highlighting what the Danish Employers' Association writes in their consultation answer as to why they support the abolition of the participation fee. They have the reason that it offers better opportunities to retain qualified foreigners on the Danish labour market. So I am glad that we are now being given the opportunity to abolish the participation fee, and I am at the same time confident that the deposit that the self-supporting students will continue to pay and which we are now raising will mean that it is motivated foreigners who are joining Danish education. Because we have had and still have a problem, I think, with too much absenteeism in Danish education. With the bill, we're gonna raise this deposit from DKK 1,250. for 2,000 kroner. per module. The 2020 Finance Act agreed that the rules on the reimbursement of deposits should be tightened up in the event of unjustified absence. The aim was to ensure that students who go to teaching take the teaching seriously and that they participate. In the context of the consultation of the draft law, significant criticism has been raised by several of the major players in the field in relation to the linking of the issue of the reimbursement of deposits and unjustified absences. The feedback was that it would pose major administrative challenges, and that is the last thing we need in Danish society. That is why I and the other parties to the financial agreement have taken note of these consultation responses and, together, we have succeeded in putting in place a model for the refund of the deposit which is easy to administer, but which continues to focus on the fact that those taking part in Danish education take the teaching seriously. In general terms, this is a model where the student loses his deposit if he or she passes the module test later than the time limits applicable to each module, but not earlier than 6 months after the start of a module. I am glad that we have been able to find such a good solution, which I think is very much in line with the criticism. It is important both for language centres and for students that this bill will come into force on 1 July. It is no secret that it has had quite serious financial consequences for our language centres that this fee was introduced a few years ago. I am therefore now looking forward to the further examination of the bill in the Folketing and, of course, I am prepared to answer any questions that may arise in connection with the examination of the bill.

ParlaMint-DK_20200514141131	The debate is open and the first rapporteur comes from the Social Democratic Party. It's sir. Rasmus Stoklund. Welcome.
ParlaMint-DK_20200514141150	Thank you. This bill establishes a new occupational scheme in which foreigners who have worked continuously for two years in Denmark can stay in Denmark for as long as they are in work. The stay is subject to three conditions: (1) that the alien is employed on proper terms and conditions of employment; (2) that they work in a full-time position; and (3) that they have worked in a full-time position with the same employer for two years. This must be fulfilled before they can extend their residence permit with this new occupational scheme. After obtaining a residence permit under the new scheme, the alien will be able to change jobs in the same industry if the new position is also under proper pay and employment conditions. In addition, the alien gets the opportunity to find a new job by innocence with a job search stay of 3 months. Thus, with the new scheme, foreigners are allowed to stay in Denmark on the condition that they are still in full-time employment at proper pay and employment conditions and thus support themselves and contribute to the common fund. The scheme is therefore in line with the principles underlying our other business schemes: you can stay in Denmark if you are in work and contribute. If you lose your job and can't find another one, you have to go home. It is important that our business community has access to relevant and qualified labour and that they have the opportunity to maintain labour in which they have benefited and benefited and invested time and resources. Social democracy therefore supports the proposal.

ParlaMint-DK_20200514154303	Thank you. There are no brief comments, so we are through the rapporteur's line, and the last person to speak in this round is the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20200514154313	I would also like to start by thanking you for a good debate. I think it's important to stick to what the point of this proposal is. It is a new residence scheme aimed at meeting the needs of business in order to maintain foreign labour • some people who are invested time and resources in. In addition, the proposal will have the effect that foreigners who have already found a foothold in the labour market and thus contribute to Danish society will have the opportunity to stay in Denmark as long as they are in work. For the government, this proposal is essentially about ensuring sensible conditions for Danish companies. We will introduce a small supplementary occupational scheme in which foreigners who have worked continuously in Denmark for two years and have been a gain for a company can apply for a work permit with a view to continuing employment. As in the case of most of the occupational schemes we have today, it will be the right of foreigners to be employed under normal pay and working conditions. This means, among other things, that there must be a full-time employment. It is important that Danish companies that have invested time and resources in the training and upgrading of a foreign employee do not simply risk losing a valued colleague, for example. because he or she divorces him or her. When a foreigner has been granted a residence permit on the new scheme, it must also be possible for the alien to change jobs in the same industry if there are equivalent terms and conditions of employment. In order to make the scheme easy and flexible to manage and at the same time support appropriate flexibility for the citizen and the business community, the concept of industry will in practice be understood as broad as possible, including both as recruitment within the same overall job category or as other employment in a company within the same industry. It can, for example, be done. be a receptionist in a hotel changing jobs to a receptionist in a hospital, or a slaughterhouse worker switching to a team leader in a slaughterhouse. In order to support the possibility of changing jobs in practice, including in the case of unemployment, foreigners living under the scheme will automatically have a job-seeking stay of 3 months if they become unemployed. I would just like to make a comment on some of what has been debated here at first reading, because I think it is important to make it clear that it has always been possible for foreigners who have withdrawn their residence permit to stay in Denmark if they have a job covered by one of our many occupational schemes. For example, you serve. more than 36,000 kroner. a month, you can get a new residence basis on the amount scheme. You can also stay if you have higher education, because then you can get a residence permit on the positive list scheme. There is nothing new about this; this has been the case for years. Thus, if you have come here as an accompanying spouse and you are divorced from your spouse, you lose your residence base as a starting point because it is linked to your spouse's residence basis, but if you have a job in Denmark where you earn more than DKK 36,000, you can get your own residence base, a new residence basis, on the amount scheme. Or if you are a refugee who gets his residence permit withdrawn because there is peace in the country of origin, because of course you lose your residence permit and are told that you have to go home, then you also have the opportunity to seek one of our business arrangements. If, for example, you're gonna have to... is a nurse, who is a job title on the positive list, you can obtain a new residence basis based on the positive list. It's perfectly normal. So we have the amount scheme, which is about making a certain amount of money, that is to say $36,000. a month, and then we have the positive list, which is about having a certain education. Now we get a system called labour relations or we get it back, you can say that you have to have had a certain number of years' seniority, namely two years' seniority. However, in my best opinion, this scheme will be used significantly less than both the positive list and the special amount scheme. It has been mentioned earlier in the debate that the last time we had this scheme, it was about ten per year that took advantage of this scheme. And it was not people who had had their residence permit withdrawn as refugees; it was people who had lost their place of residence because, for example, they had lost their place of residence. had been divorced from a spouse who had come here, and they had come as a companion. It is true that since then, fortunately, we have begun to look much more zealously at whether refugees still have a need for protection, and that is why it is of course also interesting to see whether in the future there may be some refugees or former refugees, because then they will not be refugees who will be able to avail themselves of this residence scheme. In this regard, we have tried to draw some figures on how many of the refugees who have had their residence basis taken into account in recent years have actually had two years of employment, because it is true, as has also been mentioned earlier in the debate, that, fortunately, in recent years, we have started to take seriously the fact that the refugee's residence base has to be temporary, and over a three-year period here in the last few years, from 2016 to 2018, 484 residence permits for refugees have been withdrawn, and out of the 484 there were 3 who had worked for two years with the same employer prior to the decision. On the one hand, it can be said that there are probably not so many refugees who will be using this scheme. On the other hand, it also tells us something about how incredibly sad and bad it is, unfortunately, still going away in getting newly arrived refugees into employment. As I see this new arrangement, it is not therefore a question of giving refugees a new basis for residence in Denmark. That is not at all the focus of the government. This is for us to give a little help to the business community, which is now being given the opportunity to keep a good employee in whom money, time and resources have been invested. However, I also believe that the scheme can have a positive effect on integration. That is at least what is the Danish Employers' Association's argument. They point out in their consultation answer that although the scheme can only to a very limited extent reduce the shortage of skilled labour for Danish companies, it underpins the integration efforts of companies because it creates some certainty that if they integrate a person, and it is a person who actually bites and gets two years' work, they can stay as long as they have that position or a similar position in Denmark. I think that is actually an excellent argument from the employers' association. They actually recognise that this is not what is going to solve their challenges with a shortage of skilled labour, but that it can therefore help to create some security and some security when hiring a refugee in the company. In those words, I would like to thank the rapporteurs for a good debate, I think. I am now looking forward to the further examination of the draft law, and we are, of course, prepared to answer some of the questions that may arise in this regard.

ParlaMint-DK_20200514160805	The debate is open. Sir. Rasmus Stoklund, the Social Democratic Party.
ParlaMint-DK_20200514160818	Thank you. This bill establishes an additional positive list for skilled workers from third countries in areas where there is a shortage of labour. It is a proposal which only meets employers in Denmark who already meet the requirements of proper pay and employment conditions and fulfil their obligation to train apprentices and pupils. There will therefore be strict control over those who are allowed to enter the new system. The Board of International Recruitment and Integration, SIRI, will hold quarterly meetings with the Regional Labour Market Councils, where developments in this area are followed. Cooperation shall ensure an ongoing update of the Trusted List to cover only subject areas where there is a shortage of labour. At the same time, the law tightens the level of fines for companies employing illegal labour, because it is totally unacceptable for some employers to exploit foreigners without a work permit and to hire them under completely unreasonable terms and conditions of pay and employment. That is why we think it is fair and good that in future, when this law comes into force, companies will be penalised with far more substantial fines if they use illegal labour. Access for foreign workers to Denmark must be responsible and controlled and must not impair our labour market model or lead to social dumping. With the construction of the law, we in the Social Democratic Party are confident that this business system does not do either. Social democracy therefore supports the proposal.

The idea is that Danish companies that take responsibility for society should have better opportunities to employ foreign workers in the areas where there is a shortage of labour. An important element of the bill is that the scheme is only for employers who meet the requirement for Danish pay and employment conditions and take social responsibility by fulfilling their duties regarding the training of apprentices and pupils. The hammer must therefore fall harder for those companies that violate the common rules of the game. It undermines the Danish social model if a parallel and illegal labour market occurs, where people work illegally at very low wages and under totally unacceptable conditions. In our overall assessment, these measures will lead to a better and better functioning of the labour market, more skilled labour, but also to more severe consequences for those who break the social contract. ParlaMint-DK_20200514174000	I would also like to thank you for receiving this proposal and, of course, I am pleased to note that there is support for the legislative proposal. I think I can also reveal that some of the officials who work with some of these snorkelling paragraphs are happy to clean up a little and to see today that there is support for implementing it. I would also like to acknowledge that this is all good from the sea proposal, or a pile of weddings, as I think there was a rapporteur who said. After all, it contains changes to such different laws as the Aliens Act, the Integration Act, the Birth Law, the Primary School Act, the Planning Act and various other laws. I would also like to say that I have no separate desire to make large and difficult proposals for legislation. However, I also feel responsible for making technical changes, EU implementations and clarifications as a result of inquiries by the Ombudsman in a way in which we do not spend an unnecessary amount of time, but can focus on political changes. That is actually why it is brought together in a major legislative proposal. In fact, I also have the expectation that there will be a similar bill in the next parliamentary assembly, where there will again be a number of technical changes, but if the rapporteurs have a desire to divide it up more, I think it is actually a discussion worth discussing in committee. And then I am very sensitive to putting forward the legislative proposals in a way that makes as much sense as possible to the Folketing. But it has been put forward on my part in this way in the desire that we spend our time on politics and not on dealing with a lot of more or less technical bills. I will not go through the bill in detail here from the platform, but just say that it is as a general objective to simplify the law on aliens on a number of points in order to provide greater clarity and overview. This should make the rules easier to administer for governments and authorities and, above all, easier to apply for users of the law. It can be citizens and associations, lawyers and courts and others. So it's a kind of service review of selected parts of the law. I can see that there are a number of hearing parties who are happy about that. In addition, the draft law aims to implement some recommendations from the EU, from Brussels, so as to ensure that Denmark implements, for example. the directive on broadcasting in a correct manner. The bill also contains a change to the law on plans, because several municipalities, together with the National Association of Municipalities, have asked for a solution so that the municipalities can continue with these temporary residences for refugees. I know this has been discussed in the conciliation group in the Ministry of Industry and we have agreed to implement the consensus reached there in this bill. The amendment should allow municipalities to extend derogations and permits under the Schedule to temporary residences by up to 2 years. The possibility to extend the duration is expected to be relevant in cases where the municipality needs more time to establish places of residence or where there is not expected to be the same need for places of residence after a few years. The draft law also contains a proposal for a so-called codification of existing practices so that it is directly apparent from the Aliens Act what relevant rules of the Services Act on social measures can be applied to accompanying foreign children and young people without legal residence. The reason for this part of the proposal is that the Ombudsman of the Danish Parliament has pointed out that it must be stated directly in the law, how and under what conditions coercive measures can be taken, for example. placing outside the home. The draft law ensures that the same rules are applied, as appropriate, to accompanying minors and young foreigners, i.e.: those aged between 18 and 22, whether they are legally resident in Denmark or not. I would like to conclude by thanking you for the debate on the various initiatives. As I have said, I note that there seems to be support for the bill. I look forward to further consideration and, of course, we are always available for questions and other matters.
